========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, what brings me in is... I keep hearing these very clear voices. Mostly in my apartment, but sometimes when I'm at the museum, too. You know, in those empty rooms where they keep the older exhibits. It sounds like women whispering, coming from right behind the walls, and they tell me which photographs belong in the archive. 

I used to love going to the museum when I was in college. I studied art history, you know. My favorite professor, Dr. Aris, he always wore these terrible tweed jackets that smelled like pipe tobacco and old paper. He had this massive collection of vintage postcards from the 1920s. We spent a whole semester just cataloging them. I actually found one once at a flea market that looked exactly like his house. It had a little green door and everything. I wonder whatever happened to that postcard... I think I left it in a book I donated to the library. The library downtown, not the one near the park. The one near the park always has too many pigeons out front.

Well, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I’m chosen for something incredibly important, and I have all this energy to carry out my plans. Speaking of plans, I was planning to reorganize my entire apartment yesterday because the books were all facing the wrong way. The spines have to face east, you know, towards the morning sun. He’s a new guy, always wears his hat backwards. 

But anyway, when I’m having one of those radiant days, if someone questions my plan or tells me I need to slow down and get some sleep... I become furious. Just blindly furious. It’s as if they are trying to completely erase me. My neighbor tried to tell me I was making too much noise moving the heavy oak bookcases at 3 AM, and I just couldn't believe her audacity. Well, if I look back over the last year, those bursts... you know, the days with almost no sleep, where my mind is just racing with all these brilliant plans and I end up spending so much money on supplies... and that furious energy I told you about... Oh, the light there in the late afternoon, it just coats the stone buildings in this warm, honey color. I bought a leather satchel in a little market near the Ponte Vecchio. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, actually. There was this one stretch... it must have been a few weeks, maybe a month or so ago. I wasn't feeling radiant or grand at all. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. 

Speaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.

It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.

I had a rose bush once that bloomed all through November.\n\nDoctor: How long do those radiant, high-energy periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Well, if I look back over the last year, those bursts... you know, the days with almost no sleep, where my mind is just racing with all these brilliant plans and I end up spending so much money on supplies... and that furious energy I told you about... I'd say they've taken up about six months in total. \n\nSix months is a long time, isn't it? Half a year. It reminds me of when I spent six months living in Florence right after I got my bachelor's degree. Oh, the light there in the late afternoon, it just coats the stone buildings in this warm, honey color. I bought a leather satchel in a little market near the Ponte Vecchio. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. I think I still have one of those pencils in a drawer somewhere in the kitchen. Next to the silverware. I really need to polish that silverware, it's getting tarnished. Silver polish always smells so sharp, doesn't it? It makes my nose itch just thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear the voices during times when your mood feels normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. There was this one stretch... it must have been a few weeks, maybe a month or so ago. I wasn't feeling radiant or grand at all. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. \n\nSpeaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. She would just sit in her armchair for days—this awful, scratchy floral armchair from the 1970s. It had these huge orange and brown daisies all over it. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. 

I actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. Things just aren't built to last anymore, are they? You buy an appliance and five years later it's completely useless.

Speaking of plans, I was planning to reorganize my entire apartment yesterday because the books were all facing the wrong way. The spines have to face east, you know, towards the morning sun. I don't understand why anyone would buy something with that much neon pink. Pink used to be such a delicate color, don't you think? Like a pale rose... I had a rose bush once that bloomed all through November.\n\nDoctor: How long do those radiant, high-energy periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Well, if I look back over the last year, those bursts... you know, the days with almost no sleep, where my mind is just racing with all these brilliant plans and I end up spending so much money on supplies... and that furious energy I told you about... I'd say they've taken up about six months in total. \n\nSix months is a long time, isn't it? Half a year. It reminds me of when I spent six months living in Florence right after I got my bachelor's degree. Oh, the light there in the late afternoon, it just coats the stone buildings in this warm, honey color. I bought a leather satchel in a little market near the Ponte Vecchio. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. \n\nSpeaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. She would just sit in her armchair for days—this awful, scratchy floral armchair from the 1970s. It had these huge orange and brown daisies all over it. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. \n\nI actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. It made me think of the ocean. I haven't been to the beach in absolute years. The sand always gets everywhere, doesn't it? You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. Things just aren't built to last anymore, are they? You buy an appliance and five years later it's completely useless.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid the voices and beliefs about being monitored start very suddenly, over a few days?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Suddenly? Hmm, let me think... I don't really know if it was sudden or not. It's hard to pin down exactly when things begin, isn't it? Time just sort of blends together. 

Like when my husband and I bought our first house. I remember packing all those cardboard boxes. We got them from the grocery store down the street. They smelled faintly of overripe bananas and cardboard dust. The produce manager, a lovely man named Arthur, saved them for us out back. Arthur had this magnificent mustache, thick and gray, like a walrus. He used to wax the ends of it, twirling them up into little points. It must have taken him ages every morning. I could never spend that much time in front of a mirror. I just wash my face, brush my hair, and that's that. My grandmother used to say that brushing your hair one hundred strokes before bed made it shine, but who has the energy for that? I tried it once when I was a teenager, sitting on the edge of my bed with this silver-backed brush she gave me. It had a little dent on the handle from where she dropped it on a tile floor in 1952. Tile floors are so unforgiving. Drop a glass, and it shatters into a million invisible pieces. You're finding shards for weeks, usually with your bare feet. I stepped on a piece of a broken wine glass once, right on the heel. It bled terribly. I had to wrap my foot in a tea towel until my husband could find the bandages.

been calling former colleagues, pastors, artists, and people I barely know with elaborate plans for exhibitions and youth mentorships, sometimes leaving several messages in one morning.\n- key: S041\n  name: Painful_Consequence_Behavior\n  description: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.\n  manifestation: I have spent money I needed for bills on archival boxes, printing, framing, and even a planned trip to Santo Domingo because in the moment it felt urgent and unquestionably necessary.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During the periods when I was sleeping only a few hours and racing from one archive task to another, I was also hearing the women’s voices and feeling certain the museum board was tracking my work.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: Most of these months since retirement have been dominated by stretches of feeling driven, chosen, irritable, and unable to slow down, even when I was also having quieter withdrawn days.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There was a period of several weeks when I was not especially energized or grand, but I still heard whispers in the apartment and remained convinced the arts people were monitoring my phone.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have lost the trust of colleagues, missed board meetings and bills, and needed my cousin and a museum administrator to step in because I could not manage appointments, money, or public events safely on my own.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: This whole change in me has been going on for about 10 months, starting after I retired and lost the structure of my museum schedule.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the certainty that people are watching my archive have been present for about 8 months now.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 6 months\n  manifestation: The bursts of little sleep, racing plans, spending, and furious energy have taken up about 6 of these months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S020\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. She would just sit in her armchair for days—this awful, scratchy floral armchair from the 1970s. It had these huge orange and brown daisies all over it. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. \n\nI actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. It made me think of the ocean. I haven't been to the beach in absolute years. The sand always gets everywhere, doesn't it? You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. Things just aren't built to last anymore, are they? You buy an appliance and five years later it's completely useless.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices and beliefs about being monitored start very suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Hmm, let me think... I don't really know if it was sudden or not. It's hard to pin down exactly when things begin, isn't it? Time just sort of blends together. \n\nLike when my husband and I bought our first house. I remember packing all those cardboard boxes. We got them from the grocery store down the street. They smelled faintly of overripe bananas and cardboard dust. The produce manager, a lovely man named Arthur, saved them for us out back. Arthur had this magnificent mustache, thick and gray, like a walrus. He used to wax the ends of it, twirling them up into little points. It must have taken him ages every morning. I could never spend that much time in front of a mirror. I just wash my face, brush my hair, and that's that. My grandmother used to say that brushing your hair one hundred strokes before bed made it shine, but who has the energy for that? I tried it once when I was a teenager, sitting on the edge of my bed with this silver-backed brush she gave me. It had a little dent on the handle from where she dropped it on a tile floor in 1952. Tile floors are so unforgiving. Drop a glass, and it shatters into a million invisible pieces. You're finding shards for weeks, usually with your bare feet. I stepped on a piece of a broken wine glass once, right on the heel. It bled terribly. I had to wrap my foot in a tea towel until my husband could find the bandages.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever been told you have a brain condition or another medical illness that might affect your thinking?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I've never been told I have a brain condition or anything like that. My thinking is just fine, as far as I know. My doctor back in Ohio—Dr. Miller, his name was—he always said I was perfectly healthy. He had this little office right on Main Street, next to a bakery that made the most incredible cinnamon rolls. You could smell them baking all morning. It was absolute torture sitting in the waiting room, smelling that cinnamon and sugar, especially if you hadn't had breakfast. They used this really thick cream cheese frosting, the kind that just melts into the dough when it's warm. I tried making them at home once, but the yeast didn't rise right. Baking with yeast is so temperamental, isn't it? If the water is too hot, it kills it, and if it's too cold, it just sits there doing nothing. I ended up with these dense little pucks instead of fluffy rolls. My husband ate them anyway, bless his heart. He would eat just about anything I put in front of him, even that time I accidentally used salt instead of sugar in the peach cobbler. Oh, that was a disaster. We had to throw the whole thing out for the birds, but even the crows wouldn't touch it. Crows are so smart, though. I read once that they can remember faces for years. Imagine that, a bird holding a grudge because you didn't share your sandwich with it at the park...

I studied art history, you know. My favorite professor, Dr. Aris, he always wore these terrible tweed jackets that smelled like pipe tobacco and old paper. He had this massive collection of vintage postcards from the 1920s. We spent a whole semester just cataloging them. I actually found one once at a flea market that looked exactly like his house. It had a little green door and everything. I wonder whatever happened to that postcard... I think I left it in a book I donated to the library. The library downtown, not the one near the park. The one near the park always has too many pigeons out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energized, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I’m chosen for something incredibly important, and I have all this energy to carry out my plans. Speaking of plans, I was planning to reorganize my entire apartment yesterday because the books were all facing the wrong way. The spines have to face east, you know, towards the morning sun. I read that in a design magazine once. Or maybe it was an architecture journal? I used to subscribe to so many magazines before the mail carrier started mixing them up. He’s a new guy, always wears his hat backwards. \n\nBut anyway, when I’m having one of those radiant days, if someone questions my plan or tells me I need to slow down and get some sleep... I become furious. Just blindly furious. It’s as if they are trying to completely erase me. My neighbor tried to tell me I was making too much noise moving the heavy oak bookcases at 3 AM, and I just couldn't believe her audacity. She wears these awful floral housecoats. I don't understand why anyone would buy something with that much neon pink. Pink used to be such a delicate color, don't you think? Like a pale rose... I had a rose bush once that bloomed all through November.\n\nDoctor: How long do those radiant, high-energy periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Well, if I look back over the last year, those bursts... you know, the days with almost no sleep, where my mind is just racing with all these brilliant plans and I end up spending so much money on supplies... and that furious energy I told you about... I'd say they've taken up about six months in total. \n\nSix months is a long time, isn't it? Half a year. It reminds me of when I spent six months living in Florence right after I got my bachelor's degree. Oh, the light there in the late afternoon, it just coats the stone buildings in this warm, honey color. I bought a leather satchel in a little market near the Ponte Vecchio. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. I think I still have one of those pencils in a drawer somewhere in the kitchen. Next to the silverware. I really need to polish that silverware, it's getting tarnished. Silver polish always smells so sharp, doesn't it? It makes my nose itch just thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear the voices during times when your mood feels normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. There was this one stretch... it must have been a few weeks, maybe a month or so ago. I wasn't feeling radiant or grand at all. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. \n\nSpeaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. \n\nI actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. It made me think of the ocean. I haven't been to the beach in absolute years. The sand always gets everywhere, doesn't it? You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. I think I still have one of those pencils in a drawer somewhere in the kitchen. Next to the silverware. I really need to polish that silverware, it's getting tarnished. Silver polish always smells so sharp, doesn't it? It makes my nose itch just thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear the voices during times when your mood feels normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. There was this one stretch... it must have been a few weeks, maybe a month or so ago. I wasn't feeling radiant or grand at all. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. \n\nSpeaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. She would just sit in her armchair for days—this awful, scratchy floral armchair from the 1970s. It had these huge orange and brown daisies all over it. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. \n\nI actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. It made me think of the ocean. I haven't been to the beach in absolute years. The sand always gets everywhere, doesn't it? You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. Things just aren't built to last anymore, are they? You buy an appliance and five years later it's completely useless.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices and beliefs about being monitored start very suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Hmm, let me think... I don't really know if it was sudden or not. It's hard to pin down exactly when things begin, isn't it? Time just sort of blends together. \n\nLike when my husband and I bought our first house. I remember packing all those cardboard boxes. We got them from the grocery store down the street. They smelled faintly of overripe bananas and cardboard dust. The produce manager, a lovely man named Arthur, saved them for us out back. Arthur had this magnificent mustache, thick and gray, like a walrus. He used to wax the ends of it, twirling them up into little points. It must have taken him ages every morning. I could never spend that much time in front of a mirror. I just wash my face, brush my hair, and that's that. My grandmother used to say that brushing your hair one hundred strokes before bed made it shine, but who has the energy for that? I tried it once when I was a teenager, sitting on the edge of my bed with this silver-backed brush she gave me. It had a little dent on the handle from where she dropped it on a tile floor in 1952. Tile floors are so unforgiving. Drop a glass, and it shatters into a million invisible pieces. You're finding shards for weeks, usually with your bare feet. I stepped on a piece of a broken wine glass once, right on the heel. It bled terribly. I had to wrap my foot in a tea towel until my husband could find the bandages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told you have a brain condition or another medical illness that might affect your thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told I have a brain condition or anything like that. My thinking is just fine, as far as I know. My doctor back in Ohio—Dr. Miller, his name was—he always said I was perfectly healthy. He had this little office right on Main Street, next to a bakery that made the most incredible cinnamon rolls. You could smell them baking all morning. It was absolute torture sitting in the waiting room, smelling that cinnamon and sugar, especially if you hadn't had breakfast. They used this really thick cream cheese frosting, the kind that just melts into the dough when it's warm. I tried making them at home once, but the yeast didn't rise right. Baking with yeast is so temperamental, isn't it? If the water is too hot, it kills it, and if it's too cold, it just sits there doing nothing. I ended up with these dense little pucks instead of fluffy rolls. My husband ate them anyway, bless his heart. He would eat just about anything I put in front of him, even that time I accidentally used salt instead of sugar in the peach cobbler. Oh, that was a disaster. We had to throw the whole thing out for the birds, but even the crows wouldn't touch it. Crows are so smart, though. I read once that they can remember faces for years. Imagine that, a bird holding a grudge because you didn't share your sandwich with it at the park...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, I suppose so. There was a period of several weeks, actually, where I wasn't feeling especially energized or grand at all. Just my normal self. But I still heard those whispers echoing in the apartment, and I remained completely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. It's a landline, you know. The one near the park always has too many pigeons out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energized, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I’m chosen for something incredibly important, and I have all this energy to carry out my plans. I read that in a design magazine once. Or maybe it was an architecture journal? I used to subscribe to so many magazines before the mail carrier started mixing them up. He’s a new guy, always wears his hat backwards. \n\nBut anyway, when I’m having one of those radiant days, if someone questions my plan or tells me I need to slow down and get some sleep... I become furious. Just blindly furious. It’s as if they are trying to completely erase me. My neighbor tried to tell me I was making too much noise moving the heavy oak bookcases at 3 AM, and I just couldn't believe her audacity. She wears these awful floral housecoats. I don't understand why anyone would buy something with that much neon pink. Pink used to be such a delicate color, don't you think? Like a pale rose... I had a rose bush once that bloomed all through November.\n\nDoctor: How long do those radiant, high-energy periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Well, if I look back over the last year, those bursts... you know, the days with almost no sleep, where my mind is just racing with all these brilliant plans and I end up spending so much money on supplies... and that furious energy I told you about... I'd say they've taken up about six months in total. \n\nSix months is a long time, isn't it? Half a year. It reminds me of when I spent six months living in Florence right after I got my bachelor's degree. Oh, the light there in the late afternoon, it just coats the stone buildings in this warm, honey color. I bought a leather satchel in a little market near the Ponte Vecchio. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. I think I still have one of those pencils in a drawer somewhere in the kitchen. Next to the silverware. I really need to polish that silverware, it's getting tarnished. Silver polish always smells so sharp, doesn't it? It makes my nose itch just thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear the voices during times when your mood feels normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. There was this one stretch... it must have been a few weeks, maybe a month or so ago. I wasn't feeling radiant or grand at all. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. \n\nSpeaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. She would just sit in her armchair for days—this awful, scratchy floral armchair from the 1970s. It had these huge orange and brown daisies all over it. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. \n\nI actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. It made me think of the ocean. I haven't been to the beach in absolute years. The sand always gets everywhere, doesn't it? You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. Things just aren't built to last anymore, are they? You buy an appliance and five years later it's completely useless.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices and beliefs about being monitored start very suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Hmm, let me think... I don't really know if it was sudden or not. It's hard to pin down exactly when things begin, isn't it? Time just sort of blends together. \n\nLike when my husband and I bought our first house. I remember packing all those cardboard boxes. We got them from the grocery store down the street. They smelled faintly of overripe bananas and cardboard dust. The produce manager, a lovely man named Arthur, saved them for us out back. Arthur had this magnificent mustache, thick and gray, like a walrus. He used to wax the ends of it, twirling them up into little points. It must have taken him ages every morning. I could never spend that much time in front of a mirror. I just wash my face, brush my hair, and that's that. My grandmother used to say that brushing your hair one hundred strokes before bed made it shine, but who has the energy for that? I tried it once when I was a teenager, sitting on the edge of my bed with this silver-backed brush she gave me. It had a little dent on the handle from where she dropped it on a tile floor in 1952. Tile floors are so unforgiving. Drop a glass, and it shatters into a million invisible pieces. You're finding shards for weeks, usually with your bare feet. I stepped on a piece of a broken wine glass once, right on the heel. It bled terribly. I had to wrap my foot in a tea towel until my husband could find the bandages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told you have a brain condition or another medical illness that might affect your thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told I have a brain condition or anything like that. My thinking is just fine, as far as I know. My doctor back in Ohio—Dr. Miller, his name was—he always said I was perfectly healthy. He had this little office right on Main Street, next to a bakery that made the most incredible cinnamon rolls. You could smell them baking all morning. It was absolute torture sitting in the waiting room, smelling that cinnamon and sugar, especially if you hadn't had breakfast. They used this really thick cream cheese frosting, the kind that just melts into the dough when it's warm. I tried making them at home once, but the yeast didn't rise right. Baking with yeast is so temperamental, isn't it? If the water is too hot, it kills it, and if it's too cold, it just sits there doing nothing. I ended up with these dense little pucks instead of fluffy rolls. My husband ate them anyway, bless his heart. He would eat just about anything I put in front of him, even that time I accidentally used salt instead of sugar in the peach cobbler. Oh, that was a disaster. We had to throw the whole thing out for the birds, but even the crows wouldn't touch it. Crows are so smart, though. I read once that they can remember faces for years. Imagine that, a bird holding a grudge because you didn't share your sandwich with it at the park...\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so. There was a period of several weeks, actually, where I wasn't feeling especially energized or grand at all. Just my normal self. But I still heard those whispers echoing in the apartment, and I remained completely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. It's a landline, you know. I kept it because the cordless ones always get lost in the couch cushions. \n\nMy couch has these incredibly deep cushions, stuffed with real down feathers. They're so comfortable, but the feathers do poke through the upholstery sometimes. You'll just be sitting there reading a book and suddenly you feel this sharp little prick on the back of your leg. I've tried putting a quilt over it to stop the feathers from coming out. I have this gorgeous quilt my great-aunt Martha made. It's a double wedding ring pattern, all done in scraps of old dresses from the 1930s. Some of the fabrics are so delicate now, almost like tissue paper. You really have to wash it by hand in the bathtub with a very mild soap. I use baby shampoo, actually. It smells so sweet, like lavender and chamomile. I always buy the big pump bottle because it's much more economical, and the pump makes it so easy when your hands are all wet and slippery...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I had to wrap my foot in a tea towel until my husband could find the bandages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told you have a brain condition or another medical illness that might affect your thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told I have a brain condition or anything like that. My thinking is just fine, as far as I know. My doctor back in Ohio—Dr. Miller, his name was—he always said I was perfectly healthy. He had this little office right on Main Street, next to a bakery that made the most incredible cinnamon rolls. You could smell them baking all morning. It was absolute torture sitting in the waiting room, smelling that cinnamon and sugar, especially if you hadn't had breakfast. They used this really thick cream cheese frosting, the kind that just melts into the dough when it's warm. I tried making them at home once, but the yeast didn't rise right. Baking with yeast is so temperamental, isn't it? If the water is too hot, it kills it, and if it's too cold, it just sits there doing nothing. I ended up with these dense little pucks instead of fluffy rolls. My husband ate them anyway, bless his heart. He would eat just about anything I put in front of him, even that time I accidentally used salt instead of sugar in the peach cobbler. Oh, that was a disaster. We had to throw the whole thing out for the birds, but even the crows wouldn't touch it. Crows are so smart, though. I read once that they can remember faces for years. Imagine that, a bird holding a grudge because you didn't share your sandwich with it at the park...\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so. There was a period of several weeks, actually, where I wasn't feeling especially energized or grand at all. Just my normal self. But I still heard those whispers echoing in the apartment, and I remained completely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. It's a landline, you know. I kept it because the cordless ones always get lost in the couch cushions. \n\nMy couch has these incredibly deep cushions, stuffed with real down feathers. They're so comfortable, but the feathers do poke through the upholstery sometimes. You'll just be sitting there reading a book and suddenly you feel this sharp little prick on the back of your leg. I've tried putting a quilt over it to stop the feathers from coming out. I have this gorgeous quilt my great-aunt Martha made. It's a double wedding ring pattern, all done in scraps of old dresses from the 1930s. Some of the fabrics are so delicate now, almost like tissue paper. You really have to wash it by hand in the bathtub with a very mild soap. I use baby shampoo, actually. It smells so sweet, like lavender and chamomile. I always buy the big pump bottle because it's much more economical, and the pump makes it so easy when your hands are all wet and slippery...\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high or energized most of the day nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I've been chosen for something magnificent, and the energy is just buzzing right through me from morning until night. But then, if someone questions my plan, or tells me I need to slow down, I become absolutely furious. It's as if they are trying to erase me completely. 

Speaking of erasing, I used to have this wonderful set of kneaded erasers when I was getting my degree. They were so soft, you could mold them in your hands like clay. They smelled faintly of sweet rubber and left these soft little gray smudges on your fingers. Paper was so much better back then, wasn't it? Real heavy cotton rag paper. I had this beautiful sketchbook with a dark green leather cover that I bought at a little stationery shop near the campus. The shop owner was this tiny woman who always wore these enormous, clunky silver necklaces. She had turquoise stones on them the size of robin's eggs. 

Robins are such cheerful birds, but they can be quite aggressive when they're nesting, you know. I saw one chase a squirrel right up the big oak tree in my front yard last spring. Squirrels are such a nuisance. They are always digging up the tulip bulbs I plant. I spend hours on my knees in the dirt, packing the soil just right, and the very next morning, there's a hole and the bulb is just sitting there, half-eaten on the grass. I tried sprinkling cayenne pepper on the dirt to keep them away, but then the wind blew and it got right in my eyes... it burned for hours, even after I flushed it with cold water from the garden hose. Garden hoses always have that metallic, rubbery taste if you drink from them, don't they? My brother and I used to drink from the hose all summer long when we were kids...

I'd say they've taken up about six months in total. \n\nSix months is a long time, isn't it? Half a year. It reminds me of when I spent six months living in Florence right after I got my bachelor's degree. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. I'd say they've taken up about six months in total. \n\nSix months is a long time, isn't it? Half a year. It reminds me of when I spent six months living in Florence right after I got my bachelor's degree. Oh, the light there in the late afternoon, it just coats the stone buildings in this warm, honey color. I bought a leather satchel in a little market near the Ponte Vecchio. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. I think I still have one of those pencils in a drawer somewhere in the kitchen. Next to the silverware. I really need to polish that silverware, it's getting tarnished. Silver polish always smells so sharp, doesn't it? It makes my nose itch just thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear the voices during times when your mood feels normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. There was this one stretch... it must have been a few weeks, maybe a month or so ago. I wasn't feeling radiant or grand at all. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. \n\nSpeaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. She would just sit in her armchair for days—this awful, scratchy floral armchair from the 1970s. It had these huge orange and brown daisies all over it. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. \n\nI actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. It made me think of the ocean. I haven't been to the beach in absolute years. The sand always gets everywhere, doesn't it? You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. Things just aren't built to last anymore, are they? You buy an appliance and five years later it's completely useless.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices and beliefs about being monitored start very suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Hmm, let me think... I don't really know if it was sudden or not. It's hard to pin down exactly when things begin, isn't it? Time just sort of blends together. \n\nLike when my husband and I bought our first house. I remember packing all those cardboard boxes. We got them from the grocery store down the street. They smelled faintly of overripe bananas and cardboard dust. The produce manager, a lovely man named Arthur, saved them for us out back. Arthur had this magnificent mustache, thick and gray, like a walrus. He used to wax the ends of it, twirling them up into little points. It must have taken him ages every morning. I could never spend that much time in front of a mirror. I just wash my face, brush my hair, and that's that. My grandmother used to say that brushing your hair one hundred strokes before bed made it shine, but who has the energy for that? I tried it once when I was a teenager, sitting on the edge of my bed with this silver-backed brush she gave me. It had a little dent on the handle from where she dropped it on a tile floor in 1952. Tile floors are so unforgiving. Drop a glass, and it shatters into a million invisible pieces. You're finding shards for weeks, usually with your bare feet. I stepped on a piece of a broken wine glass once, right on the heel. It bled terribly. I had to wrap my foot in a tea towel until my husband could find the bandages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told you have a brain condition or another medical illness that might affect your thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told I have a brain condition or anything like that. My thinking is just fine, as far as I know. My doctor back in Ohio—Dr. Miller, his name was—he always said I was perfectly healthy. He had this little office right on Main Street, next to a bakery that made the most incredible cinnamon rolls. You could smell them baking all morning. It was absolute torture sitting in the waiting room, smelling that cinnamon and sugar, especially if you hadn't had breakfast. They used this really thick cream cheese frosting, the kind that just melts into the dough when it's warm. I tried making them at home once, but the yeast didn't rise right. Baking with yeast is so temperamental, isn't it? If the water is too hot, it kills it, and if it's too cold, it just sits there doing nothing. I ended up with these dense little pucks instead of fluffy rolls. My husband ate them anyway, bless his heart. He would eat just about anything I put in front of him, even that time I accidentally used salt instead of sugar in the peach cobbler. Oh, that was a disaster. We had to throw the whole thing out for the birds, but even the crows wouldn't touch it. Crows are so smart, though. I read once that they can remember faces for years. Imagine that, a bird holding a grudge because you didn't share your sandwich with it at the park...\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so. There was a period of several weeks, actually, where I wasn't feeling especially energized or grand at all. Just my normal self. But I still heard those whispers echoing in the apartment, and I remained completely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. It's a landline, you know. I kept it because the cordless ones always get lost in the couch cushions. \n\nMy couch has these incredibly deep cushions, stuffed with real down feathers. They're so comfortable, but the feathers do poke through the upholstery sometimes. You'll just be sitting there reading a book and suddenly you feel this sharp little prick on the back of your leg. I've tried putting a quilt over it to stop the feathers from coming out. I have this gorgeous quilt my great-aunt Martha made. It's a double wedding ring pattern, all done in scraps of old dresses from the 1930s. Some of the fabrics are so delicate now, almost like tissue paper. You really have to wash it by hand in the bathtub with a very mild soap. I use baby shampoo, actually. It smells so sweet, like lavender and chamomile. I always buy the big pump bottle because it's much more economical, and the pump makes it so easy when your hands are all wet and slippery...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high or energized most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I've been chosen for something magnificent, and the energy is just buzzing right through me from morning until night. But then, if someone questions my plan, or tells me I need to slow down, I become absolutely furious. It's as if they are trying to erase me completely. \n\nSpeaking of erasing, I used to have this wonderful set of kneaded erasers when I was getting my degree. They were so soft, you could mold them in your hands like clay. They smelled faintly of sweet rubber and left these soft little gray smudges on your fingers. Paper was so much better back then, wasn't it? Real heavy cotton rag paper. I had this beautiful sketchbook with a dark green leather cover that I bought at a little stationery shop near the campus. The shop owner was this tiny woman who always wore these enormous, clunky silver necklaces. She had turquoise stones on them the size of robin's eggs. \n\nRobins are such cheerful birds, but they can be quite aggressive when they're nesting, you know. I saw one chase a squirrel right up the big oak tree in my front yard last spring. Squirrels are such a nuisance. They are always digging up the tulip bulbs I plant. I spend hours on my knees in the dirt, packing the soil just right, and the very next morning, there's a hole and the bulb is just sitting there, half-eaten on the grass. I tried sprinkling cayenne pepper on the dirt to keep them away, but then the wind blew and it got right in my eyes... it burned for hours, even after I flushed it with cold water from the garden hose. Garden hoses always have that metallic, rubbery taste if you drink from them, don't they? My brother and I used to drink from the hose all summer long when we were kids...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I thought I just mentioned that, Doctor. But yes, like I was saying, there was definitely a period of several weeks where I wasn't feeling grand or energized at all. Just my completely normal self. But I still heard those whispers in the apartment, and I remained absolutely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. 

The acoustics in that apartment are very strange anyway, which doesn't help. It has those high ceilings with the original plaster crown molding from the 1920s. They don't make molding like that anymore, you know. Now it's all cheap foam that they just glue right to the drywall. My uncle Arthur used to be a carpenter, and he would spend hours and hours carving wood by hand. He made me this beautiful little wooden rocking horse when I was seven. It was painted dapple gray, with real horsehair for the tail. I always wondered where he got the horsehair from. Maybe from a farm? Farms smell so earthy, like fresh hay and... well, manure, but in a good way, if that makes sense. I took a trip to a dairy farm once on a school field trip when I was a little girl. They gave us all these tiny little cartons of chocolate milk at the end of the tour. Chocolate milk always leaves that thick coating on the back of your throat, doesn't it? I prefer my milk ice cold, straight from the fridge, in a real glass, not plastic. Plastic makes everything taste like chemicals. Even the water bottles you buy at the store, if you leave them in the car on a hot day, the water tastes like melted tires...

  {
