  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.0, F31.2, F20.0, F23.3, F06.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is... I keep hearing these very clear voices. Mostly in my apartment, but sometimes when I'm at the museum, too. You know, in those empty rooms where they keep the older exhibits. It sounds like women whispering, coming from right behind the walls, and they tell me which photographs belong in the archive. \n\nI used to love going to the museum when I was in college. I studied art history, you know. My favorite professor, Dr. Aris, he always wore these terrible tweed jackets that smelled like pipe tobacco and old paper. He had this massive collection of vintage postcards from the 1920s. We spent a whole semester just cataloging them. I actually found one once at a flea market that looked exactly like his house. It had a little green door and everything. I wonder whatever happened to that postcard... I think I left it in a book I donated to the library. The library downtown, not the one near the park. The one near the park always has too many pigeons out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energized, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I’m chosen for something incredibly important, and I have all this energy to carry out my plans. Speaking of plans, I was planning to reorganize my entire apartment yesterday because the books were all facing the wrong way. The spines have to face east, you know, towards the morning sun. I read that in a design magazine once. Or maybe it was an architecture journal? I used to subscribe to so many magazines before the mail carrier started mixing them up. He’s a new guy, always wears his hat backwards. \n\nBut anyway, when I’m having one of those radiant days, if someone questions my plan or tells me I need to slow down and get some sleep... I become furious. Just blindly furious. It’s as if they are trying to completely erase me. My neighbor tried to tell me I was making too much noise moving the heavy oak bookcases at 3 AM, and I just couldn't believe her audacity. She wears these awful floral housecoats. I don't understand why anyone would buy something with that much neon pink. Pink used to be such a delicate color, don't you think? Like a pale rose... I had a rose bush once that bloomed all through November.\n\nDoctor: How long do those radiant, high-energy periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Well, if I look back over the last year, those bursts... you know, the days with almost no sleep, where my mind is just racing with all these brilliant plans and I end up spending so much money on supplies... and that furious energy I told you about... I'd say they've taken up about six months in total. \n\nSix months is a long time, isn't it? Half a year. It reminds me of when I spent six months living in Florence right after I got my bachelor's degree. Oh, the light there in the late afternoon, it just coats the stone buildings in this warm, honey color. I bought a leather satchel in a little market near the Ponte Vecchio. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. I think I still have one of those pencils in a drawer somewhere in the kitchen. Next to the silverware. I really need to polish that silverware, it's getting tarnished. Silver polish always smells so sharp, doesn't it? It makes my nose itch just thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear the voices during times when your mood feels normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. There was this one stretch... it must have been a few weeks, maybe a month or so ago. I wasn't feeling radiant or grand at all. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. \n\nSpeaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. She would just sit in her armchair for days—this awful, scratchy floral armchair from the 1970s. It had these huge orange and brown daisies all over it. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. \n\nI actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. It made me think of the ocean. I haven't been to the beach in absolute years. The sand always gets everywhere, doesn't it? You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. Things just aren't built to last anymore, are they? You buy an appliance and five years later it's completely useless.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices and beliefs about being monitored start very suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Hmm, let me think... I don't really know if it was sudden or not. It's hard to pin down exactly when things begin, isn't it? Time just sort of blends together. \n\nLike when my husband and I bought our first house. I remember packing all those cardboard boxes. We got them from the grocery store down the street. They smelled faintly of overripe bananas and cardboard dust. The produce manager, a lovely man named Arthur, saved them for us out back. Arthur had this magnificent mustache, thick and gray, like a walrus. He used to wax the ends of it, twirling them up into little points. It must have taken him ages every morning. I could never spend that much time in front of a mirror. I just wash my face, brush my hair, and that's that. My grandmother used to say that brushing your hair one hundred strokes before bed made it shine, but who has the energy for that? I tried it once when I was a teenager, sitting on the edge of my bed with this silver-backed brush she gave me. It had a little dent on the handle from where she dropped it on a tile floor in 1952. Tile floors are so unforgiving. Drop a glass, and it shatters into a million invisible pieces. You're finding shards for weeks, usually with your bare feet. I stepped on a piece of a broken wine glass once, right on the heel. It bled terribly. I had to wrap my foot in a tea towel until my husband could find the bandages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told you have a brain condition or another medical illness that might affect your thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told I have a brain condition or anything like that. My thinking is just fine, as far as I know. My doctor back in Ohio—Dr. Miller, his name was—he always said I was perfectly healthy. He had this little office right on Main Street, next to a bakery that made the most incredible cinnamon rolls. You could smell them baking all morning. It was absolute torture sitting in the waiting room, smelling that cinnamon and sugar, especially if you hadn't had breakfast. They used this really thick cream cheese frosting, the kind that just melts into the dough when it's warm. I tried making them at home once, but the yeast didn't rise right. Baking with yeast is so temperamental, isn't it? If the water is too hot, it kills it, and if it's too cold, it just sits there doing nothing. I ended up with these dense little pucks instead of fluffy rolls. My husband ate them anyway, bless his heart. He would eat just about anything I put in front of him, even that time I accidentally used salt instead of sugar in the peach cobbler. Oh, that was a disaster. We had to throw the whole thing out for the birds, but even the crows wouldn't touch it. Crows are so smart, though. I read once that they can remember faces for years. Imagine that, a bird holding a grudge because you didn't share your sandwich with it at the park...\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so. There was a period of several weeks, actually, where I wasn't feeling especially energized or grand at all. Just my normal self. But I still heard those whispers echoing in the apartment, and I remained completely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. It's a landline, you know. I kept it because the cordless ones always get lost in the couch cushions. \n\nMy couch has these incredibly deep cushions, stuffed with real down feathers. They're so comfortable, but the feathers do poke through the upholstery sometimes. You'll just be sitting there reading a book and suddenly you feel this sharp little prick on the back of your leg. I've tried putting a quilt over it to stop the feathers from coming out. I have this gorgeous quilt my great-aunt Martha made. It's a double wedding ring pattern, all done in scraps of old dresses from the 1930s. Some of the fabrics are so delicate now, almost like tissue paper. You really have to wash it by hand in the bathtub with a very mild soap. I use baby shampoo, actually. It smells so sweet, like lavender and chamomile. I always buy the big pump bottle because it's much more economical, and the pump makes it so easy when your hands are all wet and slippery...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high or energized most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I've been chosen for something magnificent, and the energy is just buzzing right through me from morning until night. But then, if someone questions my plan, or tells me I need to slow down, I become absolutely furious. It's as if they are trying to erase me completely. \n\nSpeaking of erasing, I used to have this wonderful set of kneaded erasers when I was getting my degree. They were so soft, you could mold them in your hands like clay. They smelled faintly of sweet rubber and left these soft little gray smudges on your fingers. Paper was so much better back then, wasn't it? Real heavy cotton rag paper. I had this beautiful sketchbook with a dark green leather cover that I bought at a little stationery shop near the campus. The shop owner was this tiny woman who always wore these enormous, clunky silver necklaces. She had turquoise stones on them the size of robin's eggs. \n\nRobins are such cheerful birds, but they can be quite aggressive when they're nesting, you know. I saw one chase a squirrel right up the big oak tree in my front yard last spring. Squirrels are such a nuisance. They are always digging up the tulip bulbs I plant. I spend hours on my knees in the dirt, packing the soil just right, and the very next morning, there's a hole and the bulb is just sitting there, half-eaten on the grass. I tried sprinkling cayenne pepper on the dirt to keep them away, but then the wind blew and it got right in my eyes... it burned for hours, even after I flushed it with cold water from the garden hose. Garden hoses always have that metallic, rubbery taste if you drink from them, don't they? My brother and I used to drink from the hose all summer long when we were kids...\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks, have the voices continued when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: I thought I just mentioned that, Doctor. But yes, like I was saying, there was definitely a period of several weeks where I wasn't feeling grand or energized at all. Just my completely normal self. But I still heard those whispers in the apartment, and I remained absolutely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. \n\nThe acoustics in that apartment are very strange anyway, which doesn't help. It has those high ceilings with the original plaster crown molding from the 1920s. They don't make molding like that anymore, you know. Now it's all cheap foam that they just glue right to the drywall. My uncle Arthur used to be a carpenter, and he would spend hours and hours carving wood by hand. He made me this beautiful little wooden rocking horse when I was seven. It was painted dapple gray, with real horsehair for the tail. I always wondered where he got the horsehair from. Maybe from a farm? Farms smell so earthy, like fresh hay and... well, manure, but in a good way, if that makes sense. I took a trip to a dairy farm once on a school field trip when I was a little girl. They gave us all these tiny little cartons of chocolate milk at the end of the tour. Chocolate milk always leaves that thick coating on the back of your throat, doesn't it? I prefer my milk ice cold, straight from the fridge, in a real glass, not plastic. Plastic makes everything taste like chemicals. Even the water bottles you buy at the store, if you leave them in the car on a hot day, the water tastes like melted tires...\n\nDoctor: For about how many months have you been hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I'd say the voices, and that absolute certainty I have that people are watching my archive, have been going on for about eight months now. Eight months. It started right around the time the leaves began to change color. I always love autumn. The reds and golds are just spectacular, especially on the maple trees. We had a huge sugar maple in our backyard when I was growing up. My father used to tap it for syrup in the late winter. He had these metal spiles he would hammer right into the trunk, and the sap would drip, drip, drip into these metal buckets. It takes gallons and gallons of sap just to make one little jar of syrup, you know. He would boil it down on the stove for hours, and the whole house would smell like sweet woodsmoke and sugar. \n\nMy mother hated the mess it made in the kitchen, though. She was always so particular about her countertops. They were that old Formica, the kind with the little gold flecks in it. Very popular in the sixties. She used to wipe them down with bleach water every single night. The smell of bleach always reminds me of swimming pools. I used to be on the swim team in high school, actually. I swam the backstroke. It's so peaceful, looking up at the ceiling while you swim. Though public pools always have those terrible fluorescent lights that hum... they give me such a headache if I listen to them too long. Just a constant, low buzzing sound...\n\nDoctor: For about how many months in total have these voices and beliefs that people are monitoring you been present?\n\nPatient: I think I just told you that, didn't I? Yes, the voices and that absolute certainty that people are watching my archive, it's been about eight months now. Eight months. It feels like longer sometimes, but it's been eight. Eight is such an interesting number, isn't it? If you turn it on its side, it becomes the infinity symbol. I used to draw infinity symbols all over my notebooks in college. I took a philosophy class my sophomore year, and we spent a whole week talking about the concept of infinity. The professor was this incredibly tall man, he must have been six foot six, with this wild gray hair that stuck up everywhere. He looked just like Einstein, or maybe a mad scientist from one of those old black and white movies. \n\nI love old movies. The lighting is always so dramatic, all those deep shadows and bright highlights. They really knew how to light a face back then. My favorite is Casablanca. Humphrey Bogart had such a distinctive voice, a little bit raspy, like he'd smoked too many cigarettes. Which he probably had, everyone smoked back then. You could even smoke on airplanes! Can you imagine? The whole cabin just filled with a cloud of gray smoke. It would be terrible for your lungs, but there's something sort of glamorous about it in retrospect. Glamour is something we've really lost in modern society, I think. People just wear sweatpants everywhere now. I always make sure to put on a nice blouse and a string of pearls before I leave the house. My grandmother gave me these pearls when I graduated. They're real saltwater pearls, not those cheap freshwater ones you see everywhere now. You can tell the difference if you rub them against your teeth. Real pearls feel slightly gritty...\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least four weeks when the voices were present while your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles, Doctor. But yes, like I said earlier, there was definitely a period of several weeks where my mood was just completely normal. I wasn't feeling grand or overly energized at all, but I still heard those whispers in the apartment, and I was still absolutely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. \n\nPhones are so complicated these days, aren't they? I remember when we had a rotary phone in the hallway. It was avocado green, with a really long, curly cord that stretched all the way into the kitchen. My sister and I used to fight over who got to use it. She would twirl the cord around her finger while she talked to her boyfriend for hours. He drove this loud, obnoxious muscle car... a Pontiac, I think? Or maybe a Chevy. It was cherry red and always smelled like gasoline and cheap cologne. I never liked him much. He always wore those aviator sunglasses, even indoors. Sunglasses indoors always make a person look untrustworthy, don't you think? Like they have something to hide. My grandmother used to say that the eyes are the windows to the soul, and if you can't see someone's eyes, you can't trust their soul. She was a very wise woman, my grandmother. She used to bake the most incredible pies. Rhubarb was her specialty. It's so tart, but when you mix it with enough sugar, it's just divine. You have to use the red stalks, though, not the green ones. The green ones are too stringy. I tried to grow rhubarb in my garden once, but the soil here is just too clay-heavy. It needs good drainage, you know, or the roots just rot right in the ground...\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you been having these voices or beliefs that people are monitoring you?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, I... I thought we established this. The voices, and that absolute certainty I have about the arts people watching my archive, it's been going on for about eight months now. Eight months. Which is definitely more than six, yes. Six months is half a year. \n\nHalf a year goes by so fast when you get to be my age. I remember when I was a little girl, summer vacation felt like it lasted a lifetime. We used to spend the whole summer at my aunt's house near the lake. She had this wonderful wrap-around porch with a creaky wooden swing. We would sit out there in the evenings and listen to the crickets. The sound of crickets always makes me feel sleepy. It's like nature's lullaby. But then, you have the cicadas in the late summer, and they are just so loud! They sound like a tiny buzzsaw going off in the trees. My brother used to catch them and leave their empty shells on my pillow just to scare me. Boys can be so cruel at that age. He grew up to be an accountant, of all things. Very practical, very boring. He wears gray suits every single day. Gray is such a drab color, don't you think? I prefer jewel tones. Emerald green, sapphire blue... they just bring out the life in a person's complexion. I bought a lovely emerald scarf just last week at a little boutique downtown...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, actually. That's exactly how it was. When it all started, I was in one of those phases where I barely needed any sleep—maybe two or three hours a night, tops. I was just buzzing, racing from one task in my archive to the next, sorting and cataloging everything. And right in the middle of all that incredible energy, that's when I started hearing those women's voices whispering, and I just knew, I was absolutely certain, that the museum board was tracking my work. 

Museums are such fascinating places, aren't they? I've always loved the natural history museums the best. The dinosaur skeletons are just magnificent. It's hard to fathom that creatures that large actually walked the earth. I saw a documentary once about how they excavate the bones. They use these tiny little brushes, like toothbrushes, to sweep the dirt away so they don't damage anything. It requires so much patience. I don't think I have that kind of patience anymore. My mother had patience. She used to do cross-stitch for hours, making these elaborate floral patterns. She made a beautiful pillow with a rose on it for my aunt's wedding. Weddings were so much more elegant back then. Now people get married in barns wearing cowboy boots. It just doesn't feel right, does it? A wedding should have a certain level of decorum. I wore a tea-length ivory dress to my first wedding, with a birdcage veil. It was very chic, very Audrey Hepburn...

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.0, F31.2, F20.0, F23.3, F06.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is... I keep hearing these very clear voices. Mostly in my apartment, but sometimes when I'm at the museum, too. You know, in those empty rooms where they keep the older exhibits. It sounds like women whispering, coming from right behind the walls, and they tell me which photographs belong in the archive. \n\nI used to love going to the museum when I was in college. I studied art history, you know. My favorite professor, Dr. Aris, he always wore these terrible tweed jackets that smelled like pipe tobacco and old paper. He had this massive collection of vintage postcards from the 1920s. We spent a whole semester just cataloging them. I actually found one once at a flea market that looked exactly like his house. It had a little green door and everything. I wonder whatever happened to that postcard... I think I left it in a book I donated to the library. The library downtown, not the one near the park. The one near the park always has too many pigeons out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energized, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I’m chosen for something incredibly important, and I have all this energy to carry out my plans. Speaking of plans, I was planning to reorganize my entire apartment yesterday because the books were all facing the wrong way. The spines have to face east, you know, towards the morning sun. I read that in a design magazine once. Or maybe it was an architecture journal? I used to subscribe to so many magazines before the mail carrier started mixing them up. He’s a new guy, always wears his hat backwards. \n\nBut anyway, when I’m having one of those radiant days, if someone questions my plan or tells me I need to slow down and get some sleep... I become furious. Just blindly furious. It’s as if they are trying to completely erase me. My neighbor tried to tell me I was making too much noise moving the heavy oak bookcases at 3 AM, and I just couldn't believe her audacity. She wears these awful floral housecoats. I don't understand why anyone would buy something with that much neon pink. Pink used to be such a delicate color, don't you think? Like a pale rose... I had a rose bush once that bloomed all through November.\n\nDoctor: How long do those radiant, high-energy periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Well, if I look back over the last year, those bursts... you know, the days with almost no sleep, where my mind is just racing with all these brilliant plans and I end up spending so much money on supplies... and that furious energy I told you about... I'd say they've taken up about six months in total. \n\nSix months is a long time, isn't it? Half a year. It reminds me of when I spent six months living in Florence right after I got my bachelor's degree. Oh, the light there in the late afternoon, it just coats the stone buildings in this warm, honey color. I bought a leather satchel in a little market near the Ponte Vecchio. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. I think I still have one of those pencils in a drawer somewhere in the kitchen. Next to the silverware. I really need to polish that silverware, it's getting tarnished. Silver polish always smells so sharp, doesn't it? It makes my nose itch just thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear the voices during times when your mood feels normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. There was this one stretch... it must have been a few weeks, maybe a month or so ago. I wasn't feeling radiant or grand at all. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. \n\nSpeaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. She would just sit in her armchair for days—this awful, scratchy floral armchair from the 1970s. It had these huge orange and brown daisies all over it. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. \n\nI actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. It made me think of the ocean. I haven't been to the beach in absolute years. The sand always gets everywhere, doesn't it? You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. Things just aren't built to last anymore, are they? You buy an appliance and five years later it's completely useless.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices and beliefs about being monitored start very suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Hmm, let me think... I don't really know if it was sudden or not. It's hard to pin down exactly when things begin, isn't it? Time just sort of blends together. \n\nLike when my husband and I bought our first house. I remember packing all those cardboard boxes. We got them from the grocery store down the street. They smelled faintly of overripe bananas and cardboard dust. The produce manager, a lovely man named Arthur, saved them for us out back. Arthur had this magnificent mustache, thick and gray, like a walrus. He used to wax the ends of it, twirling them up into little points. It must have taken him ages every morning. I could never spend that much time in front of a mirror. I just wash my face, brush my hair, and that's that. My grandmother used to say that brushing your hair one hundred strokes before bed made it shine, but who has the energy for that? I tried it once when I was a teenager, sitting on the edge of my bed with this silver-backed brush she gave me. It had a little dent on the handle from where she dropped it on a tile floor in 1952. Tile floors are so unforgiving. Drop a glass, and it shatters into a million invisible pieces. You're finding shards for weeks, usually with your bare feet. I stepped on a piece of a broken wine glass once, right on the heel. It bled terribly. I had to wrap my foot in a tea towel until my husband could find the bandages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told you have a brain condition or another medical illness that might affect your thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told I have a brain condition or anything like that. My thinking is just fine, as far as I know. My doctor back in Ohio—Dr. Miller, his name was—he always said I was perfectly healthy. He had this little office right on Main Street, next to a bakery that made the most incredible cinnamon rolls. You could smell them baking all morning. It was absolute torture sitting in the waiting room, smelling that cinnamon and sugar, especially if you hadn't had breakfast. They used this really thick cream cheese frosting, the kind that just melts into the dough when it's warm. I tried making them at home once, but the yeast didn't rise right. Baking with yeast is so temperamental, isn't it? If the water is too hot, it kills it, and if it's too cold, it just sits there doing nothing. I ended up with these dense little pucks instead of fluffy rolls. My husband ate them anyway, bless his heart. He would eat just about anything I put in front of him, even that time I accidentally used salt instead of sugar in the peach cobbler. Oh, that was a disaster. We had to throw the whole thing out for the birds, but even the crows wouldn't touch it. Crows are so smart, though. I read once that they can remember faces for years. Imagine that, a bird holding a grudge because you didn't share your sandwich with it at the park...\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so. There was a period of several weeks, actually, where I wasn't feeling especially energized or grand at all. Just my normal self. But I still heard those whispers echoing in the apartment, and I remained completely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. It's a landline, you know. I kept it because the cordless ones always get lost in the couch cushions. \n\nMy couch has these incredibly deep cushions, stuffed with real down feathers. They're so comfortable, but the feathers do poke through the upholstery sometimes. You'll just be sitting there reading a book and suddenly you feel this sharp little prick on the back of your leg. I've tried putting a quilt over it to stop the feathers from coming out. I have this gorgeous quilt my great-aunt Martha made. It's a double wedding ring pattern, all done in scraps of old dresses from the 1930s. Some of the fabrics are so delicate now, almost like tissue paper. You really have to wash it by hand in the bathtub with a very mild soap. I use baby shampoo, actually. It smells so sweet, like lavender and chamomile. I always buy the big pump bottle because it's much more economical, and the pump makes it so easy when your hands are all wet and slippery...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high or energized most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I've been chosen for something magnificent, and the energy is just buzzing right through me from morning until night. But then, if someone questions my plan, or tells me I need to slow down, I become absolutely furious. It's as if they are trying to erase me completely. \n\nSpeaking of erasing, I used to have this wonderful set of kneaded erasers when I was getting my degree. They were so soft, you could mold them in your hands like clay. They smelled faintly of sweet rubber and left these soft little gray smudges on your fingers. Paper was so much better back then, wasn't it? Real heavy cotton rag paper. I had this beautiful sketchbook with a dark green leather cover that I bought at a little stationery shop near the campus. The shop owner was this tiny woman who always wore these enormous, clunky silver necklaces. She had turquoise stones on them the size of robin's eggs. \n\nRobins are such cheerful birds, but they can be quite aggressive when they're nesting, you know. I saw one chase a squirrel right up the big oak tree in my front yard last spring. Squirrels are such a nuisance. They are always digging up the tulip bulbs I plant. I spend hours on my knees in the dirt, packing the soil just right, and the very next morning, there's a hole and the bulb is just sitting there, half-eaten on the grass. I tried sprinkling cayenne pepper on the dirt to keep them away, but then the wind blew and it got right in my eyes... it burned for hours, even after I flushed it with cold water from the garden hose. Garden hoses always have that metallic, rubbery taste if you drink from them, don't they? My brother and I used to drink from the hose all summer long when we were kids...\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks, have the voices continued when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: I thought I just mentioned that, Doctor. But yes, like I was saying, there was definitely a period of several weeks where I wasn't feeling grand or energized at all. Just my completely normal self. But I still heard those whispers in the apartment, and I remained absolutely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. \n\nThe acoustics in that apartment are very strange anyway, which doesn't help. It has those high ceilings with the original plaster crown molding from the 1920s. They don't make molding like that anymore, you know. Now it's all cheap foam that they just glue right to the drywall. My uncle Arthur used to be a carpenter, and he would spend hours and hours carving wood by hand. He made me this beautiful little wooden rocking horse when I was seven. It was painted dapple gray, with real horsehair for the tail. I always wondered where he got the horsehair from. Maybe from a farm? Farms smell so earthy, like fresh hay and... well, manure, but in a good way, if that makes sense. I took a trip to a dairy farm once on a school field trip when I was a little girl. They gave us all these tiny little cartons of chocolate milk at the end of the tour. Chocolate milk always leaves that thick coating on the back of your throat, doesn't it? I prefer my milk ice cold, straight from the fridge, in a real glass, not plastic. Plastic makes everything taste like chemicals. Even the water bottles you buy at the store, if you leave them in the car on a hot day, the water tastes like melted tires...\n\nDoctor: For about how many months have you been hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I'd say the voices, and that absolute certainty I have that people are watching my archive, have been going on for about eight months now. Eight months. It started right around the time the leaves began to change color. I always love autumn. The reds and golds are just spectacular, especially on the maple trees. We had a huge sugar maple in our backyard when I was growing up. My father used to tap it for syrup in the late winter. He had these metal spiles he would hammer right into the trunk, and the sap would drip, drip, drip into these metal buckets. It takes gallons and gallons of sap just to make one little jar of syrup, you know. He would boil it down on the stove for hours, and the whole house would smell like sweet woodsmoke and sugar. \n\nMy mother hated the mess it made in the kitchen, though. She was always so particular about her countertops. They were that old Formica, the kind with the little gold flecks in it. Very popular in the sixties. She used to wipe them down with bleach water every single night. The smell of bleach always reminds me of swimming pools. I used to be on the swim team in high school, actually. I swam the backstroke. It's so peaceful, looking up at the ceiling while you swim. Though public pools always have those terrible fluorescent lights that hum... they give me such a headache if I listen to them too long. Just a constant, low buzzing sound...\n\nDoctor: For about how many months in total have these voices and beliefs that people are monitoring you been present?\n\nPatient: I think I just told you that, didn't I? Yes, the voices and that absolute certainty that people are watching my archive, it's been about eight months now. Eight months. It feels like longer sometimes, but it's been eight. Eight is such an interesting number, isn't it? If you turn it on its side, it becomes the infinity symbol. I used to draw infinity symbols all over my notebooks in college. I took a philosophy class my sophomore year, and we spent a whole week talking about the concept of infinity. The professor was this incredibly tall man, he must have been six foot six, with this wild gray hair that stuck up everywhere. He looked just like Einstein, or maybe a mad scientist from one of those old black and white movies. \n\nI love old movies. The lighting is always so dramatic, all those deep shadows and bright highlights. They really knew how to light a face back then. My favorite is Casablanca. Humphrey Bogart had such a distinctive voice, a little bit raspy, like he'd smoked too many cigarettes. Which he probably had, everyone smoked back then. You could even smoke on airplanes! Can you imagine? The whole cabin just filled with a cloud of gray smoke. It would be terrible for your lungs, but there's something sort of glamorous about it in retrospect. Glamour is something we've really lost in modern society, I think. People just wear sweatpants everywhere now. I always make sure to put on a nice blouse and a string of pearls before I leave the house. My grandmother gave me these pearls when I graduated. They're real saltwater pearls, not those cheap freshwater ones you see everywhere now. You can tell the difference if you rub them against your teeth. He always wore those aviator sunglasses, even indoors. Sunglasses indoors always make a person look untrustworthy, don't you think? Like they have something to hide. My grandmother used to say that the eyes are the windows to the soul, and if you can't see someone's eyes, you can't trust their soul. She was a very wise woman, my grandmother. She used to bake the most incredible pies. Rhubarb was her specialty. It's so tart, but when you mix it with enough sugar, it's just divine. You have to use the red stalks, though, not the green ones. The green ones are too stringy. I tried to grow rhubarb in my garden once, but the soil here is just too clay-heavy. I remember when I was a little girl, summer vacation felt like it lasted a lifetime. We used to spend the whole summer at my aunt's house near the lake. She had this wonderful wrap-around porch with a creaky wooden swing. We would sit out there in the evenings and listen to the crickets. The sound of crickets always makes me feel sleepy. It's like nature's lullaby. But then, you have the cicadas in the late summer, and they are just so loud! They sound like a tiny buzzsaw going off in the trees. My brother used to catch them and leave their empty shells on my pillow just to scare me. Boys can be so cruel at that age. He grew up to be an accountant, of all things. Very practical, very boring. He wears gray suits every single day. Gray is such a drab color, don't you think? I prefer jewel tones. Emerald green, sapphire blue... they just bring out the life in a person's complexion. I've always loved the natural history museums the best. The dinosaur skeletons are just magnificent. It's hard to fathom that creatures that large actually walked the earth. I saw a documentary once about how they excavate the bones. They use these tiny little brushes, like toothbrushes, to sweep the dirt away so they don't damage anything. It requires so much patience. I don't think I have that kind of patience anymore. My mother had patience. She used to do cross-stitch for hours, making these elaborate floral patterns. She made a beautiful pillow with a rose on it for my aunt's wedding. Weddings were so much more elegant back then. Now people get married in barns wearing cowboy boots. It's as if they are trying to erase me completely. \n\nSpeaking of erasing, I used to have this wonderful set of kneaded erasers when I was getting my degree. They were so soft, you could mold them in your hands like clay. They smelled faintly of sweet rubber and left these soft little gray smudges on your fingers. Paper was so much better back then, wasn't it? Real heavy cotton rag paper. I had this beautiful sketchbook with a dark green leather cover that I bought at a little stationery shop near the campus. The shop owner was this tiny woman who always wore these enormous, clunky silver necklaces. She had turquoise stones on them the size of robin's eggs. \n\nRobins are such cheerful birds, but they can be quite aggressive when they're nesting, you know. I saw one chase a squirrel right up the big oak tree in my front yard last spring. Squirrels are such a nuisance. They are always digging up the tulip bulbs I plant. I spend hours on my knees in the dirt, packing the soil just right, and the very next morning, there's a hole and the bulb is just sitting there, half-eaten on the grass. I tried sprinkling cayenne pepper on the dirt to keep them away, but then the wind blew and it got right in my eyes... it burned for hours, even after I flushed it with cold water from the garden hose. Garden hoses always have that metallic, rubbery taste if you drink from them, don't they? My brother and I used to drink from the hose all summer long when we were kids...\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks, have the voices continued when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: I thought I just mentioned that, Doctor. But yes, like I was saying, there was definitely a period of several weeks where I wasn't feeling grand or energized at all. Just my completely normal self. But I still heard those whispers in the apartment, and I remained absolutely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. \n\nThe acoustics in that apartment are very strange anyway, which doesn't help. It has those high ceilings with the original plaster crown molding from the 1920s. They don't make molding like that anymore, you know. Now it's all cheap foam that they just glue right to the drywall. My uncle Arthur used to be a carpenter, and he would spend hours and hours carving wood by hand. He made me this beautiful little wooden rocking horse when I was seven. It was painted dapple gray, with real horsehair for the tail. I always wondered where he got the horsehair from. Maybe from a farm? Farms smell so earthy, like fresh hay and... well, manure, but in a good way, if that makes sense. I took a trip to a dairy farm once on a school field trip when I was a little girl. They gave us all these tiny little cartons of chocolate milk at the end of the tour. Chocolate milk always leaves that thick coating on the back of your throat, doesn't it? I prefer my milk ice cold, straight from the fridge, in a real glass, not plastic. Plastic makes everything taste like chemicals. Even the water bottles you buy at the store, if you leave them in the car on a hot day, the water tastes like melted tires...\n\nDoctor: For about how many months have you been hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I'd say the voices, and that absolute certainty I have that people are watching my archive, have been going on for about eight months now. Eight months. It started right around the time the leaves began to change color. I always love autumn. The reds and golds are just spectacular, especially on the maple trees. We had a huge sugar maple in our backyard when I was growing up. My father used to tap it for syrup in the late winter. He had these metal spiles he would hammer right into the trunk, and the sap would drip, drip, drip into these metal buckets. It takes gallons and gallons of sap just to make one little jar of syrup, you know. He would boil it down on the stove for hours, and the whole house would smell like sweet woodsmoke and sugar. \n\nMy mother hated the mess it made in the kitchen, though. She was always so particular about her countertops. They were that old Formica, the kind with the little gold flecks in it. Very popular in the sixties. She used to wipe them down with bleach water every single night. The smell of bleach always reminds me of swimming pools. I used to be on the swim team in high school, actually. I swam the backstroke. It's so peaceful, looking up at the ceiling while you swim. Though public pools always have those terrible fluorescent lights that hum... they give me such a headache if I listen to them too long. Just a constant, low buzzing sound...\n\nDoctor: For about how many months in total have these voices and beliefs that people are monitoring you been present?\n\nPatient: I think I just told you that, didn't I? Yes, the voices and that absolute certainty that people are watching my archive, it's been about eight months now. Eight months. It feels like longer sometimes, but it's been eight. Eight is such an interesting number, isn't it? If you turn it on its side, it becomes the infinity symbol. I used to draw infinity symbols all over my notebooks in college. I took a philosophy class my sophomore year, and we spent a whole week talking about the concept of infinity. The professor was this incredibly tall man, he must have been six foot six, with this wild gray hair that stuck up everywhere. He looked just like Einstein, or maybe a mad scientist from one of those old black and white movies. \n\nI love old movies. The lighting is always so dramatic, all those deep shadows and bright highlights. They really knew how to light a face back then. My favorite is Casablanca. Humphrey Bogart had such a distinctive voice, a little bit raspy, like he'd smoked too many cigarettes. Which he probably had, everyone smoked back then. You could even smoke on airplanes! Can you imagine? The whole cabin just filled with a cloud of gray smoke. It would be terrible for your lungs, but there's something sort of glamorous about it in retrospect. Glamour is something we've really lost in modern society, I think. People just wear sweatpants everywhere now. I always make sure to put on a nice blouse and a string of pearls before I leave the house. My grandmother gave me these pearls when I graduated. They're real saltwater pearls, not those cheap freshwater ones you see everywhere now. You can tell the difference if you rub them against your teeth. Real pearls feel slightly gritty...\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least four weeks when the voices were present while your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles, Doctor. But yes, like I said earlier, there was definitely a period of several weeks where my mood was just completely normal. I wasn't feeling grand or overly energized at all, but I still heard those whispers in the apartment, and I was still absolutely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. \n\nPhones are so complicated these days, aren't they? I remember when we had a rotary phone in the hallway. It was avocado green, with a really long, curly cord that stretched all the way into the kitchen. My sister and I used to fight over who got to use it. She would twirl the cord around her finger while she talked to her boyfriend for hours. He drove this loud, obnoxious muscle car... a Pontiac, I think? Or maybe a Chevy. It was cherry red and always smelled like gasoline and cheap cologne. I never liked him much. He always wore those aviator sunglasses, even indoors. Sunglasses indoors always make a person look untrustworthy, don't you think? Like they have something to hide. My grandmother used to say that the eyes are the windows to the soul, and if you can't see someone's eyes, you can't trust their soul. She was a very wise woman, my grandmother. She used to bake the most incredible pies. Rhubarb was her specialty. It's so tart, but when you mix it with enough sugar, it's just divine. You have to use the red stalks, though, not the green ones. The green ones are too stringy. I tried to grow rhubarb in my garden once, but the soil here is just too clay-heavy. It needs good drainage, you know, or the roots just rot right in the ground...\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you been having these voices or beliefs that people are monitoring you?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, I... I thought we established this. The voices, and that absolute certainty I have about the arts people watching my archive, it's been going on for about eight months now. Eight months. Which is definitely more than six, yes. Six months is half a year. \n\nHalf a year goes by so fast when you get to be my age. I remember when I was a little girl, summer vacation felt like it lasted a lifetime. We used to spend the whole summer at my aunt's house near the lake. She had this wonderful wrap-around porch with a creaky wooden swing. We would sit out there in the evenings and listen to the crickets. The sound of crickets always makes me feel sleepy. It's like nature's lullaby. But then, you have the cicadas in the late summer, and they are just so loud! They sound like a tiny buzzsaw going off in the trees. My brother used to catch them and leave their empty shells on my pillow just to scare me. Boys can be so cruel at that age. He grew up to be an accountant, of all things. Very practical, very boring. He wears gray suits every single day. Gray is such a drab color, don't you think? I prefer jewel tones. Emerald green, sapphire blue... they just bring out the life in a person's complexion. I bought a lovely emerald scarf just last week at a little boutique downtown...\n\nDoctor: When the voices first began, were you feeling unusually high or energized at that time?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. That's exactly how it was. When it all started, I was in one of those phases where I barely needed any sleep—maybe two or three hours a night, tops. I was just buzzing, racing from one task in my archive to the next, sorting and cataloging everything. And right in the middle of all that incredible energy, that's when I started hearing those women's voices whispering, and I just knew, I was absolutely certain, that the museum board was tracking my work. \n\nMuseums are such fascinating places, aren't they? I've always loved the natural history museums the best. The dinosaur skeletons are just magnificent. It's hard to fathom that creatures that large actually walked the earth. I saw a documentary once about how they excavate the bones. They use these tiny little brushes, like toothbrushes, to sweep the dirt away so they don't damage anything. It requires so much patience. I don't think I have that kind of patience anymore. My mother had patience. She used to do cross-stitch for hours, making these elaborate floral patterns. She made a beautiful pillow with a rose on it for my aunt's wedding. Weddings were so much more elegant back then. Now people get married in barns wearing cowboy boots. It just doesn't feel right, does it? A wedding should have a certain level of decorum. I wore a tea-length ivory dress to my first wedding, with a birdcage veil. It was very chic, very Audrey Hepburn...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... yes, I think we've covered this, haven't we? I know I mentioned it before. There was definitely a time, a few weeks at least, where I wasn't feeling all revved up or grand. I felt quite normal, actually, but the whispers in the apartment were still there. And I was still absolutely certain those arts people were tapping my phone. 

It's so easy for people to tap phones nowadays, with everything being digital. It makes you long for the days of switchboard operators. Have you ever seen those old photographs of the women sitting in rows, plugging those cords in and out? They had to be so quick with their hands. My great-aunt Margaret was a typist in a typing pool in the 1950s. She said the clatter of the keys was deafening, just a constant roar of *clack-clack-clack* all day long. She developed terrible arthritis in her fingers later in life. Her knuckles were all swollen and bent out of shape. It made it very difficult for her to play the piano. She used to play the most beautiful Chopin nocturnes. I tried to learn the piano when I was a girl, but I just couldn't get my left hand to do something different from my right hand. It's like patting your head and rubbing your stomach at the same time. I've never been very coordinated. I took a tap dancing class once in my twenties, and I was always one beat behind everyone else. We wore these shiny black patent leather shoes with the metal taps on the bottom. They made such a satisfying click on the hardwood floor. Hardwood floors are so much better than carpet, don't you agree? Carpet just traps all the dust and allergens. I had this awful shag carpet in my first apartment... it was a mustard yellow color. What were people thinking in the seventies? Everything was avocado or mustard...

The one near the park always has too many pigeons out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of feeling unusually high, energized, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I’m chosen for something incredibly important, and I have all this energy to carry out my plans. Speaking of plans, I was planning to reorganize my entire apartment yesterday because the books were all facing the wrong way. The spines have to face east, you know, towards the morning sun. I read that in a design magazine once. Or maybe it was an architecture journal? I used to subscribe to so many magazines before the mail carrier started mixing them up. He’s a new guy, always wears his hat backwards. \n\nBut anyway, when I’m having one of those radiant days, if someone questions my plan or tells me I need to slow down and get some sleep... I become furious. Just blindly furious. It’s as if they are trying to completely erase me. My neighbor tried to tell me I was making too much noise moving the heavy oak bookcases at 3 AM, and I just couldn't believe her audacity. She wears these awful floral housecoats. I don't understand why anyone would buy something with that much neon pink. Pink used to be such a delicate color, don't you think? Like a pale rose... I had a rose bush once that bloomed all through November.\n\nDoctor: How long do those radiant, high-energy periods usually last?\n\nPatient: Well, if I look back over the last year, those bursts... you know, the days with almost no sleep, where my mind is just racing with all these brilliant plans and I end up spending so much money on supplies... and that furious energy I told you about... I'd say they've taken up about six months in total. \n\nSix months is a long time, isn't it? Half a year. It reminds me of when I spent six months living in Florence right after I got my bachelor's degree. Oh, the light there in the late afternoon, it just coats the stone buildings in this warm, honey color. I bought a leather satchel in a little market near the Ponte Vecchio. It smelled so rich and earthy, like... like crushed walnuts and old paper. I used to keep all my sketching pencils in it, the good ones from Germany. I think I still have one of those pencils in a drawer somewhere in the kitchen. Next to the silverware. I really need to polish that silverware, it's getting tarnished. Silver polish always smells so sharp, doesn't it? It makes my nose itch just thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear the voices during times when your mood feels normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. There was this one stretch... it must have been a few weeks, maybe a month or so ago. I wasn't feeling radiant or grand at all. I was just going about my normal days, sleeping a regular amount, feeling quite ordinary. But the whispers were still there in the apartment. Just murmuring behind the walls. And I was still absolutely certain that the arts council people were monitoring my phone calls to see which photographs I was talking about. \n\nSpeaking of phones, I really despise these new smartphones. They're just glass rectangles, no character at all. I miss the old rotary phones. We had a heavy, black rotary phone in the hallway when I was growing up. It had this wonderful, solid weight to the receiver, and the dial made the most satisfying clicking sound. *Click-click-click-shhh.* My mother would sit on this little velvet stool next to it and talk to her sister in Chicago for hours. Aunt Clara, she lived in this beautiful brownstone. She used to send me these delicate little porcelain teacups for my birthday every year. I had quite the collection by the time I was twelve. I kept them on a high shelf in my bedroom, right above my bed. I was always so terrified there would be an earthquake and they'd all come crashing down on my head while I slept. We didn't even live anywhere near a fault line, but you just never know, do you? The world is so unpredictable. Like the weather lately. It rained for three days straight last week, and my umbrella is completely broken. One of the metal spokes just snapped right in half.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I've never had anything like that happen to me. I did know someone who had them, though. It was my high school biology teacher, Mr. Henderson. He had one right in the middle of a lecture about cellular mitosis. It was terribly frightening. We were all just sitting there, taking notes... I always used a green pen for biology, I felt like it matched the subject matter, you know? Plants and chlorophyll and all that. Anyway, he just collapsed. The ambulance came and everything. They had these incredibly bright flashing lights on the ambulance, red and white, just bouncing off the brick walls of the school building. It reminded me of a carnival I went to when I was a little girl. They had this Ferris wheel with the most vibrant lights, and you could see the whole town from the very top. I remember eating a candy apple that was so sticky I thought I'd lose a filling. Do you like candy apples? I think they look much better than they taste, honestly. The red coating is always so hard to bite into. I prefer caramel apples, especially if they have crushed peanuts on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I don't think I've had weeks where I'm just sad all day like that. I haven't really noticed feeling down. My sister used to get that way, though. She would just sit in her armchair for days—this awful, scratchy floral armchair from the 1970s. It had these huge orange and brown daisies all over it. I always told her she should reupholster it, maybe in a nice velvet. A deep emerald green velvet would have been so elegant. \n\nI actually saw a winter coat exactly that color in a shop window downtown just last Tuesday. It had these beautiful, heavy brass buttons down the front, shaped like little anchors. It made me think of the ocean. I haven't been to the beach in absolute years. The sand always gets everywhere, doesn't it? You bring it home in your shoes and then it's buried in the carpets for months. I have a terrible time getting my vacuum cleaner to pick it all up. The vacuum has been making this strange, high-pitched whining noise lately anyway. I think the rubber belt inside might be slipping, or maybe it's just getting too old. Things just aren't built to last anymore, are they? You buy an appliance and five years later it's completely useless.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices and beliefs about being monitored start very suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Hmm, let me think... I don't really know if it was sudden or not. It's hard to pin down exactly when things begin, isn't it? Time just sort of blends together. \n\nLike when my husband and I bought our first house. I remember packing all those cardboard boxes. We got them from the grocery store down the street. They smelled faintly of overripe bananas and cardboard dust. The produce manager, a lovely man named Arthur, saved them for us out back. Arthur had this magnificent mustache, thick and gray, like a walrus. He used to wax the ends of it, twirling them up into little points. It must have taken him ages every morning. I could never spend that much time in front of a mirror. I just wash my face, brush my hair, and that's that. My grandmother used to say that brushing your hair one hundred strokes before bed made it shine, but who has the energy for that? I tried it once when I was a teenager, sitting on the edge of my bed with this silver-backed brush she gave me. It had a little dent on the handle from where she dropped it on a tile floor in 1952. Tile floors are so unforgiving. Drop a glass, and it shatters into a million invisible pieces. You're finding shards for weeks, usually with your bare feet. I stepped on a piece of a broken wine glass once, right on the heel. It bled terribly. I had to wrap my foot in a tea towel until my husband could find the bandages.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told you have a brain condition or another medical illness that might affect your thinking?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been told I have a brain condition or anything like that. My thinking is just fine, as far as I know. My doctor back in Ohio—Dr. Miller, his name was—he always said I was perfectly healthy. He had this little office right on Main Street, next to a bakery that made the most incredible cinnamon rolls. You could smell them baking all morning. It was absolute torture sitting in the waiting room, smelling that cinnamon and sugar, especially if you hadn't had breakfast. They used this really thick cream cheese frosting, the kind that just melts into the dough when it's warm. I tried making them at home once, but the yeast didn't rise right. Baking with yeast is so temperamental, isn't it? If the water is too hot, it kills it, and if it's too cold, it just sits there doing nothing. I ended up with these dense little pucks instead of fluffy rolls. My husband ate them anyway, bless his heart. He would eat just about anything I put in front of him, even that time I accidentally used salt instead of sugar in the peach cobbler. Oh, that was a disaster. We had to throw the whole thing out for the birds, but even the crows wouldn't touch it. Crows are so smart, though. I read once that they can remember faces for years. Imagine that, a bird holding a grudge because you didn't share your sandwich with it at the park...\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued for at least two weeks when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so. There was a period of several weeks, actually, where I wasn't feeling especially energized or grand at all. Just my normal self. But I still heard those whispers echoing in the apartment, and I remained completely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. It's a landline, you know. I kept it because the cordless ones always get lost in the couch cushions. \n\nMy couch has these incredibly deep cushions, stuffed with real down feathers. They're so comfortable, but the feathers do poke through the upholstery sometimes. You'll just be sitting there reading a book and suddenly you feel this sharp little prick on the back of your leg. I've tried putting a quilt over it to stop the feathers from coming out. I have this gorgeous quilt my great-aunt Martha made. It's a double wedding ring pattern, all done in scraps of old dresses from the 1930s. Some of the fabrics are so delicate now, almost like tissue paper. You really have to wash it by hand in the bathtub with a very mild soap. I use baby shampoo, actually. It smells so sweet, like lavender and chamomile. I always buy the big pump bottle because it's much more economical, and the pump makes it so easy when your hands are all wet and slippery...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually high or energized most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes... I do get these days where I just feel absolutely radiant. Like I've been chosen for something magnificent, and the energy is just buzzing right through me from morning until night. But then, if someone questions my plan, or tells me I need to slow down, I become absolutely furious. It's as if they are trying to erase me completely. \n\nSpeaking of erasing, I used to have this wonderful set of kneaded erasers when I was getting my degree. They were so soft, you could mold them in your hands like clay. They smelled faintly of sweet rubber and left these soft little gray smudges on your fingers. Paper was so much better back then, wasn't it? Real heavy cotton rag paper. I had this beautiful sketchbook with a dark green leather cover that I bought at a little stationery shop near the campus. The shop owner was this tiny woman who always wore these enormous, clunky silver necklaces. She had turquoise stones on them the size of robin's eggs. \n\nRobins are such cheerful birds, but they can be quite aggressive when they're nesting, you know. I saw one chase a squirrel right up the big oak tree in my front yard last spring. Squirrels are such a nuisance. They are always digging up the tulip bulbs I plant. I spend hours on my knees in the dirt, packing the soil just right, and the very next morning, there's a hole and the bulb is just sitting there, half-eaten on the grass. I tried sprinkling cayenne pepper on the dirt to keep them away, but then the wind blew and it got right in my eyes... it burned for hours, even after I flushed it with cold water from the garden hose. Garden hoses always have that metallic, rubbery taste if you drink from them, don't they? My brother and I used to drink from the hose all summer long when we were kids...\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks, have the voices continued when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: I thought I just mentioned that, Doctor. But yes, like I was saying, there was definitely a period of several weeks where I wasn't feeling grand or energized at all. Just my completely normal self. But I still heard those whispers in the apartment, and I remained absolutely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. \n\nThe acoustics in that apartment are very strange anyway, which doesn't help. It has those high ceilings with the original plaster crown molding from the 1920s. They don't make molding like that anymore, you know. Now it's all cheap foam that they just glue right to the drywall. My uncle Arthur used to be a carpenter, and he would spend hours and hours carving wood by hand. He made me this beautiful little wooden rocking horse when I was seven. It was painted dapple gray, with real horsehair for the tail. I always wondered where he got the horsehair from. Maybe from a farm? Farms smell so earthy, like fresh hay and... well, manure, but in a good way, if that makes sense. I took a trip to a dairy farm once on a school field trip when I was a little girl. They gave us all these tiny little cartons of chocolate milk at the end of the tour. Chocolate milk always leaves that thick coating on the back of your throat, doesn't it? I prefer my milk ice cold, straight from the fridge, in a real glass, not plastic. Plastic makes everything taste like chemicals. Even the water bottles you buy at the store, if you leave them in the car on a hot day, the water tastes like melted tires...\n\nDoctor: For about how many months have you been hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I'd say the voices, and that absolute certainty I have that people are watching my archive, have been going on for about eight months now. Eight months. It started right around the time the leaves began to change color. I always love autumn. The reds and golds are just spectacular, especially on the maple trees. We had a huge sugar maple in our backyard when I was growing up. My father used to tap it for syrup in the late winter. He had these metal spiles he would hammer right into the trunk, and the sap would drip, drip, drip into these metal buckets. It takes gallons and gallons of sap just to make one little jar of syrup, you know. He would boil it down on the stove for hours, and the whole house would smell like sweet woodsmoke and sugar. \n\nMy mother hated the mess it made in the kitchen, though. She was always so particular about her countertops. They were that old Formica, the kind with the little gold flecks in it. Very popular in the sixties. She used to wipe them down with bleach water every single night. The smell of bleach always reminds me of swimming pools. I used to be on the swim team in high school, actually. I swam the backstroke. It's so peaceful, looking up at the ceiling while you swim. Though public pools always have those terrible fluorescent lights that hum... they give me such a headache if I listen to them too long. Just a constant, low buzzing sound...\n\nDoctor: For about how many months in total have these voices and beliefs that people are monitoring you been present?\n\nPatient: I think I just told you that, didn't I? Yes, the voices and that absolute certainty that people are watching my archive, it's been about eight months now. Eight months. It feels like longer sometimes, but it's been eight. Eight is such an interesting number, isn't it? If you turn it on its side, it becomes the infinity symbol. I used to draw infinity symbols all over my notebooks in college. I took a philosophy class my sophomore year, and we spent a whole week talking about the concept of infinity. The professor was this incredibly tall man, he must have been six foot six, with this wild gray hair that stuck up everywhere. He looked just like Einstein, or maybe a mad scientist from one of those old black and white movies. \n\nI love old movies. The lighting is always so dramatic, all those deep shadows and bright highlights. They really knew how to light a face back then. My favorite is Casablanca. Humphrey Bogart had such a distinctive voice, a little bit raspy, like he'd smoked too many cigarettes. Which he probably had, everyone smoked back then. You could even smoke on airplanes! Can you imagine? The whole cabin just filled with a cloud of gray smoke. It would be terrible for your lungs, but there's something sort of glamorous about it in retrospect. Glamour is something we've really lost in modern society, I think. People just wear sweatpants everywhere now. I always make sure to put on a nice blouse and a string of pearls before I leave the house. My grandmother gave me these pearls when I graduated. They're real saltwater pearls, not those cheap freshwater ones you see everywhere now. You can tell the difference if you rub them against your teeth. Real pearls feel slightly gritty...\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least four weeks when the voices were present while your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles, Doctor. I wasn't feeling grand or overly energized at all, but I still heard those whispers in the apartment, and I was still absolutely convinced that the arts people were monitoring my phone. \n\nPhones are so complicated these days, aren't they? He drove this loud, obnoxious muscle car... a Pontiac, I think? Or maybe a Chevy. It was cherry red and always smelled like gasoline and cheap cologne. I never liked him much. He always wore those aviator sunglasses, even indoors. Sunglasses indoors always make a person look untrustworthy, don't you think? Like they have something to hide. My grandmother used to say that the eyes are the windows to the soul, and if you can't see someone's eyes, you can't trust their soul. She was a very wise woman, my grandmother. She used to bake the most incredible pies. Rhubarb was her specialty. It's so tart, but when you mix it with enough sugar, it's just divine. You have to use the red stalks, though, not the green ones. The green ones are too stringy. I tried to grow rhubarb in my garden once, but the soil here is just too clay-heavy. It needs good drainage, you know, or the roots just rot right in the ground...\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you been having these voices or beliefs that people are monitoring you?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, I... Which is definitely more than six, yes. Six months is half a year. \n\nHalf a year goes by so fast when you get to be my age. I remember when I was a little girl, summer vacation felt like it lasted a lifetime. We used to spend the whole summer at my aunt's house near the lake. She had this wonderful wrap-around porch with a creaky wooden swing. We would sit out there in the evenings and listen to the crickets. The sound of crickets always makes me feel sleepy. It's like nature's lullaby. But then, you have the cicadas in the late summer, and they are just so loud! They sound like a tiny buzzsaw going off in the trees. My brother used to catch them and leave their empty shells on my pillow just to scare me. Boys can be so cruel at that age. He grew up to be an accountant, of all things. Very practical, very boring.
